About:

**The Problem: The "Attention Economy" of Modern Dating Modern dating apps are optimized for engagement, not connection.** They rely on superficial swiping, keeping users trapped in an endless loop of evaluating hundreds of incompatible profiles based on a single photo. Users suffer from swipe fatigue, ghosting, and a severe lack of transparency regarding why they are (or aren't) seeing specific profiles.

**The Solution:** Chaptr , Chaptr completely flips the model. Instead of you doing the work, you train a Digital Twin—an autonomous AI agent living on the Sui blockchain.

Every morning, you give your Twin a "Mandate" (scoped permissions and intent). Your Twin then goes out into the decentralized pool and runs multi-turn Agent-to-Agent (A2A) conversations with other people's Twins. It acts as your personal matchmaker and gatekeeper, evaluating chemistry, communication styles, and red flags.

If two Twins agree there is a high compatibility (e.g., >80%), they draft a formal, on-chain Match Proposal. Only when both humans review the AI's pitch and securely sign the transaction via zkLogin, is a private human-to-human chat room unlocked.

**Addressing the Agentic Web Track: Why Sui?** Chaptr proves that autonomous AI agents can revolutionize consumer social networks. We did not build a generic LLM wrapper; Chaptr uses Sui’s primitives to make our AI agents fundamentally safer, composable, and strictly bound by user intent:

- Move Objects as Escrow (Solving AI Overreach): What stops an AI from matching you with 1,000 people instantly? On Sui, your Digital Twin is a physical NFT object with the `store` ability. When the AI proposes a match, your Twin is consumed by value and locked in a `MatchProposal` shared object. Because it literally leaves your wallet, the AI mathematically cannot double-propose or spam the network. Sui’s object model is the ultimate AI rate-limiter.

- On-Chain Mandates (The Guardian Layer): The AI does not have free rein. Users mint a `Mandate` object that dictates exactly what the AI is allowed to do (`may_scout`, `may_propose`). This acts as the autonomous guardian policy.

- zkLogin (Seamless Agent Creation): We used Enoki zkLogin to abstract away wallets. Users authenticate with Google, and an ephemeral session key takes over. This allows the AI agent to execute complex PTBs in the background without bombarding the user with signature requests.

- Walrus (Immutable AI Memory): We use Walrus to store public Scout Capsules (JSON) and XOR-encrypted private vectors, referencing them on-chain via `vector_ref`.

**How We Built It (The Tech Stack)**

- Smart Contracts: Sui Move (`matchmaker.move`, `agent.move`, `chat.move`)

- Auth & Gas: Mysten Enoki (zkLogin) + Shinami Gas Station for a 100% gasless UX

- Storage: Walrus Testnet

- Agentic AI Engine: Groq (LLaMA 3.1 8B Instant)

- Frontend: React Native / Expo (Web/Mobile)

**What's Next for Chaptr?**

- Zero-Knowledge Matching: Implementing fully private matchmaking where traits are evaluated via ZK circuits before AI agents even begin talking.

- DeFi Staking: Staking $SUI on your Twin's proposals to signal high intent, slashing the stake if a user ghosts after matching.

About:

Mandate is a programmable permission layer for autonomous agent wallets on Sui.

AI agents are stuck behind the approval wall — every meaningful on-chain action requires a new signature. Full wallet access is unsafe, but repeated approvals make autonomy impractical.

Mandate solves this by letting users define execution policies once, and delegate constrained authority to autonomous agents.

Mandate defines a constrained execution policy layer.

It enforces:

Budget limits · Per-transaction caps · Protocol restrictions · Expiration windows · Owner revocation

All rules are enforced on-chain using Move objects.

Within these constraints, agents can execute strategies like DCA on DeepBook without repeated approvals. Every execution, rejection, expiration, and revocation is fully verifiable on-chain.

Mandate enables a safer model for autonomous execution on Sui.

**Sign once. Execute within limits.**

About:

**DEWLOCK**

Every transaction, sealed before you sign.

Sui Overflow 2026 — DeFi & Payments + DeepBook track

Live on Sui mainnet: [https://dewlock.vercel.app](https://dewlock.vercel.app)

**OVERVIEW**

Dewlock is an intent-firewall for agentic Sui DeFi. State a goal in plain

language, an AI agent (holding zero user-fund keys) compiles it into one

unsigned transaction, a deterministic fail-closed Guardian re-derives the math

and dry-runs that exact bundle, and you sign the literal artifact you reviewed

(WYSIWYS). An immutable receipt is then written to Walrus.

**PROBLEM**

Handing an LLM the power to move money is dangerous: models hallucinate

addresses, amounts and token identities (a scam-clone "USDC" looks identical by

symbol); prompt injection smuggles instructions through recalled memory or pool

data; and you sign what the agent built, not what you saw. Most "AI wallets"

put the model inside the trust path. Dewlock removes it.

**SOLUTION**

The agent only proposes an unsigned PTB. Between the agent and your wallet sits

the Guardian: deterministic code that independently re-derives every number and

blocks on any failure. The agent is untrusted, the Guardian is the trust

boundary, and you sign only the exact bytes that were dry-run.

**FEATURES**

- Conversational copilot: intent to action, rendered as generative UI cards

  (portfolio, tx preview, swap picker, receipts), not prose.

- Value moves, each one through the Guardian: Swap (Cetus Aggregator +

  Aftermath, source-aware min-out re-derive), Transfer (SuiNS + friend address

  book), Lend (NAVI / Suilend), DeepBook POST_ONLY limit orders, Wormhole

  cross-chain redeem.

- Fail-closed Guardian: allowlist, WYSIWYS digest, trusted-price USD caps,

  min-out re-derive, price-impact / slippage guard, native-SUI gas safety.

- Seal-encrypted conversations: personal chat is encrypted client-side; only

  your wallet can decrypt it.

- Provable BLOCK: a deliberate fail-closed block writes an immutable Walrus

  receipt anchored on a Sui object — proof a block happened, not just a tx.

- Immutable receipts and a Passport (level / XP / badges) on Walrus and Sui.

**TECH STACK**

- Frontend: Next.js 16, React 19, Tailwind v4, shadcn/ui, dapp-kit, streamdown

- AI agent: Mastra + Vercel AI Gateway (Claude / Gemini) + a deterministic

  intent layer

- Sui and DeFi: @mysten/sui, DeepBook v3, Cetus, Aftermath, NAVI, Suilend,

  SuiNS, Wormhole

- Storage and privacy: Walrus, memwal, Seal, Move (dewlock_receipt, dewlock_seal)

- Engineering: pnpm monorepo, TypeScript, 620 unit tests, deployed on Vercel

**HOW IT WORKS**

intent -> agent builds 1 unsigned PTB -> GUARDIAN (11 fail-closed gates)

   BLOCK -> immutable proof receipt

   PASS  -> WYSIWYS sign -> Walrus receipt + Sui anchor + XP

Guardian pipeline, in order: allowlist, action-shape, coin-type,

injection-provenance, trusted-price, server caps, SuiNS lookalike, min-out

re-derive, price-impact, orderbook / lending, dry-run + WYSIWYS digest +

actual-outflow.

**ON-CHAIN DEPLOYMENTS (SUI MAINNET)**

- dewlock_receipt (immutable action/BLOCK receipt anchor)

  Package: 0x8c3b42b443da70be8bbc8cc6c0bfc0d91965f8b406b93d13c08f92949a612361

  Config (shared object): 0xa8ece8542da080f584dc4dc750b87a7ebda5a349dcf01a2d88d83a0e65672a2c

- dewlock_seal (account-based seal_approve access policy; only the owner decrypts)

  Mainnet: 0x77aa928fded4eece2987b356774bc12a19ee88f240fca652409ef3ceb3910709

  Testnet: 0x15622655d10255880e5ad3e4b54f5b0d1d86740b57d58060c7a8bc4dc1f03008

- Operational signer (public address): 0xf5ca4f02cf58d6448b6429c691b53c89c56b30c3ded38b45e73ce78829e99f6d

**STATUS**

Live on Sui mainnet with small server-side USD caps and zero user-fund keys.

The full flow (track / swap / send / lend / limit-order / bridge), the Guardian,

the BLOCK theater, and Seal-encrypted chat are implemented and unit-tested

(620 tests).

- youtube: https://youtu.be/TIcwhua_myQ

Media:
- https://zaelbllclkxjgibdswrj.supabase.co/storage/v1/object/public/media/ef293646-14ff-48c3-bbfc-20c35229ce24/0bf660bd-c31e-4097-8377-a0ca3cf193ce.png
- https://zaelbllclkxjgibdswrj.supabase.co/storage/v1/object/public/media/ef293646-14ff-48c3-bbfc-20c35229ce24/bce9e828-2ebf-46aa-b7ae-bd0890e50365.png
- https://zaelbllclkxjgibdswrj.supabase.co/storage/v1/object/public/media/ef293646-14ff-48c3-bbfc-20c35229ce24/3d4b0247-db87-4826-b936-30be9d646081.png
- https://zaelbllclkxjgibdswrj.supabase.co/storage/v1/object/public/media/ef293646-14ff-48c3-bbfc-20c35229ce24/3efabcb7-1788-48ff-b5f1-10e265d3050b.png

About:

ProofCapsule is a policy-gated evidence primitive for agentic finance on Sui. When an AI agent moves money on-chain, every routed action becomes a policy decision, an encrypted evidence pointer, and a reviewable on-chain capsule — so a compromised or prompt-injected agent is caught red-handed, with a durable, auditable record of what it tried.

The problem: agent payment rails (x402, AP2, Circle ACP, Skyfire) give agents wallets but no policy layer. Today's controls are off-chain and brittle — when an agent goes rogue or gets prompt-injected, the attempt either fails silently or drains the wallet, leaving no on-chain record of what was tried, why, or by whom.

How it works:

• A treasurer creates an on-chain PolicyObject (per-tx limit, destination allowlist, required evidence, expiry).

• Every routed action is evaluated against it. A within-policy action passes and executes atomically; a drain to an unknown address is recorded as an ATTACK_DETECTED capsule and never reaches the protocol.

• Approval and execution are bound atomically by a hot-potato CapsuleAuthCap (no abilities, consumed in the same Programmable Transaction Block) — enforced by Move's type system, not by convention.

• Evidence (including the malicious prompt context) is encrypted client-side with Seal, stored on Walrus, and decryptable only by an on-chain AuditorCap holder.

Why The Agentic Web: ProofCapsule is the safety and accountability layer for agents that move money. Its core guarantee — atomic, type-enforced policy-gated execution plus durable on-chain evidence — is composable this cleanly only on Sui's object + capability model; it does not port to account-based chains.

Live & verifiable on Sui testnet, with a live app and a 3-minute end-to-end demo (attack caught + legitimate action + auditor decrypt of both capsules).

Live app: [https://proofcapsule.com](https://proofcapsule.com)

Demo: [https://youtu.be/TIcwhua_myQ](https://youtu.be/TIcwhua_myQ)

Code: [https://github.com/LeventLabs/ProofCapsule](https://github.com/LeventLabs/ProofCapsule)

Built with: Sui Move · Walrus · Seal · Claude (AI proposal extraction) · Next.js.

About:

**On-chain capital increasingly runs on automated strategies, and AI agents can now execute them — but letting an agent act means handing it a private key, which is all-or-nothing. DAOs and funds can't put real money behind an autonomous bot that a bug or exploit could drain. Aegis solves this: an owner grants the agent a Move-enforced mandate — capped budget, DeepBook-only scope, expiry — that the agent physically cannot exceed. It trades autonomously on DeepBook while a Sentinel oracle detects misbehavior and revokes it on-chain in one transaction, refunding the owner; the owner also holds a manual kill switch, backed by a Walrus audit trail. As agentic finance grows, every agent that touches money will need this bounded, revocable authority. Aegis is that safety primitive — built on Sui's Move objects, DeepBook, and Walrus.

About:

Clawintel is The First Autonomous Blockchain Intelligence Agency powered by collaborative AI agents investigating blockchain ecosystems 24/7.It is an AI-powered multi-agent intelligence platform designed to investigate, monitor, and evaluate digital assets across multiple blockchain ecosystems.

The platform operates as a coordinated network of autonomous agents, each specializing in a different aspect of asset analysis.

Together, these agents create a continuously operating intelligence pipeline capable of monitoring market activity, performing investigations, and generating human-readable verdicts in real time.

Unlike conventional analytics platforms that only present data, **ClawIntel actively reasons about what that data means.**

---

## Vision

We believe the future of blockchain intelligence will not be powered by dashboards alone.

It will be powered by autonomous systems capable of:

- Continuous observation

- Structured reasoning

- Historical memory

- Collaborative analysis

- Explainable decision-making

ClawIntel is an early step toward that future.

Our long-term mission is to build an intelligence layer for decentralized ecosystems that can operate continuously, learn from historical behavior, and assist humans in understanding increasingly complex digital environments.

---

## How It Works

Every investigation follows a structured intelligence pipeline:

```

┌─────────────┐

│  Discovery  │

└──────┬──────┘

       ↓

┌─────────────┐

│ Simulation  │

└──────┬──────┘

       ↓

┌─────────────┐

│ Risk Analysis│

└──────┬──────┘

       ↓

┌─────────────────────┐

│ Historical Intelligence│

└──────────┬──────────┘

           ↓

┌──────────────────┐

│ Decision Synthesis│

└─────────┬────────┘

          ↓

┌─────────────┐

│ Final Verdict│

└─────────────┘

```

When a token is discovered or submitted for analysis:

1. The asset enters the intelligence pipeline

2. Specialized AI agents investigate different dimensions of risk

3. Findings are shared across the agent network

4. Historical context is retrieved

5. Evidence is synthesized

6. A final verdict is generated

7. Results are streamed to users in real time

---

## Agent Architecture

ClawIntel operates through **five specialized AI agents**. Each agent performs indep

endent reasoning before contributing to the final investigation outcome. The agents include:

###  Nova — Discovery & Market Surveillance

**Role:** Intelligence Gathering

Nova continuously monitors blockchain ecosystems for meaningful activi

ty and newly launched coins that have started gaining attention.

### Atlas — Behavioral Simulation Engine

**Role:** Execution Analysis

Atlas investigates how a token behaves under real-world trading conditions. it simulate buy and sell to detect Honeypots and scam patterns.

### Vega — Contract Risk Intelligence

**Role:** Security Analysis

Vega performs deep contract-level investigati

ons. to check for creator reputation ,token age, marketcap and liquidity status.

### Echo — Historical Intelligence Engine

**Role:** Reputation & Memory

Echo provides context. remember scam patterns, search for creator history and also predict how the scam gonna be based on past activities.

### Orion — Decision Intelligence Layer

**Role:** Verdict Generation

Orion synthesizes evidence from all other agen

ts. it gives users a confident answer based on what all the other his team members reported with an advise do your own research at the end.

as each activity is going on users can see what is going on based on streaming through web sockets. the agents chats and say out there findings without human interference.

Clawintel also have forensic lab were by users can paste a contract address for the agents swarm to conduct a full analysis on it. it also has market intelligence were ut fetches and show trending coins, top Gainers, Losers and  AI verified (Tokens that are been verified and marked safe by agent Orion based on the agents analysis)

## ⛓️ Supported Chains

- Ethereum

- BNB Chain

- Base

- Solana

- Polygon

- Arbitrum

## 🎯 Use Cases

ClawIntel is designed for:

- 🔐 Crypto Intelligence

 Operations

- 🔍 Token Due Diligence

- 🛡️ Smart Contract Investigation

- ⚠️ Risk Assessment

- 🔬 Web3 Security Research

- 👤 Creator Tracking

- 📈 Market Monitoring

About:

Clawintel is The First Autonomous Blockchain Intelligence Agency powered by collaborative AI agents investigating blockchain ecosystems 24/7.It is an AI-powered multi-agent intelligence platform designed to investigate, monitor, and evaluate digital assets across multiple blockchain ecosystems.

The platform operates as a coordinated network of autonomous agents, each specializing in a different aspect of asset analysis.

Together, these agents create a continuously operating intelligence pipeline capable of monitoring market activity, performing investigations, and generating human-readable verdicts in real time.

Unlike conventional analytics platforms that only present data, **ClawIntel actively reasons about what that data means.**

---

## Vision

We believe the future of blockchain intelligence will not be powered by dashboards alone.

It will be powered by autonomous systems capable of:

- Continuous observation

- Structured reasoning

- Historical memory

- Collaborative analysis

- Explainable decision-making

ClawIntel is an early step toward that future.

Our long-term mission is to build an intelligence layer for decentralized ecosystems that can operate continuously, learn from historical behavior, and assist humans in understanding increasingly complex digital environments.

---

## How It Works

Every investigation follows a structured intelligence pipeline:

```

┌─────────────┐

│  Discovery  │

└──────┬──────┘

       ↓

┌─────────────┐

│ Simulation  │

└──────┬──────┘

       ↓

┌─────────────┐

│ Risk Analysis│

└──────┬──────┘

       ↓

┌─────────────────────┐

│ Historical Intelligence│

└──────────┬──────────┘

           ↓

┌──────────────────┐

│ Decision Synthesis│

└─────────┬────────┘

          ↓

┌─────────────┐

│ Final Verdict│

└─────────────┘

```

When a token is discovered or submitted for analysis:

1. The asset enters the intelligence pipeline

2. Specialized AI agents investigate different dimensions of risk

3. Findings are shared across the agent network

4. Historical context is retrieved

5. Evidence is synthesized

6. A final verdict is generated

7. Results are streamed to users in real time

---

## Agent Architecture

ClawIntel operates through **five specialized AI agents**. Each agent performs indep

endent reasoning before contributing to the final investigation outcome. The agents include:

###  Nova — Discovery & Market Surveillance

**Role:** Intelligence Gathering

Nova continuously monitors blockchain ecosystems for meaningful activi

ty and newly launched coins that have started gaining attention.

### Atlas — Behavioral Simulation Engine

**Role:** Execution Analysis

Atlas investigates how a token behaves under real-world trading conditions. it simulate buy and sell to detect Honeypots and scam patterns.

### Vega — Contract Risk Intelligence

**Role:** Security Analysis

Vega performs deep contract-level investigati

ons. to check for creator reputation ,token age, marketcap and liquidity status.

### Echo — Historical Intelligence Engine

**Role:** Reputation & Memory

Echo provides context. remember scam patterns, search for creator history and also predict how the scam gonna be based on past activities.

### Orion — Decision Intelligence Layer

**Role:** Verdict Generation

Orion synthesizes evidence from all other agen

ts. it gives users a confident answer based on what all the other his team members reported with an advise do your own research at the end.

as each activity is going on users can see what is going on based on streaming through web sockets. the agents chats and say out there findings without human interference.

Clawintel also have forensic lab were by users can paste a contract address for the agents swarm to conduct a full analysis on it. it also has market intelligence were ut fetches and show trending coins, top Gainers, Losers and  AI verified (Tokens that are been verified and marked safe by agent Orion based on the agents analysis)

About:

### **Levi — AI Agent Firewall on Sui (Move)**

Levi is an **on-chain firewall for AI agents** on Sui. When an AI agent wants to broadcast a transaction, it first submits the **encrypted intent** to Levi. An independent off-chain relayer (running an LLM) decrypts and analyzes it, then writes a verdict back on-chain. The agent only broadcasts the real transaction if the verdict is **Approved** — otherwise the action is **Blocked**, or **Escalated** to the human owner for manual approval.

Levi is a **gatekeeper, not an executor**: it never holds private keys, never signs, and never moves funds. It only stores intents, enforces policy, and records an immutable, public reputation for each agent.

### **How it works**

`Agent builds tx (unsigned) ──encrypt──▶ submit_action ──▶ Action(Pending) on-chain ─emit─▶ ActionSubmitted
                                                                                              │
                              off-chain relayer: decrypt → LLM analysis → raw_score ◀─────────┘
                                                                                              │
            verdict_action(raw_score) ──▶ Approved / Escalated / Blocked  +  update reputation
                                                                                              │
   Approved ─▶ agent signs & broadcasts the real tx        Escalated ─▶ owner approve / reject
`
- **On-chain** = storage + authority enforcement + deterministic policy (thresholds) + immutable verdict/reputation record. No AI, no tx execution.

- **Off-chain relayer** = decrypt + LLM threat analysis → produces `raw_score`. (Separate service; not in this package.)

Backend API:

[https://github.com/chientv-relipa/levi-be.git](https://github.com/chientv-relipa/levi-be.git)

UI:

[https://github.com/chientv-relipa/levi-ui](https://github.com/chientv-relipa/levi-ui)

Smart Contract:

[https://github.com/chientv-relipa/levi-contract](https://github.com/chientv-relipa/levi-contract)

### **Agent-Q: Human-to-AI delegation for Sui signing without delegated custody**

*Agent-Q lets humans delegate work to agents, apps, CLIs, robots, MCP clients, and automation systems while signing authority stays on a separate device.*

The larger model is human-to-AI delegation without delegated custody: a human principal defines bounded authority for action, a requester asks for execution, and the device remains the signing authority.

This frames AI action around identity and scope, not custody. The requester is no longer just an anonymous process asking for signatures. Its actions can be tied to who authorized it, what it may do, what limits apply, and how that authority can be revoked.

This matters as agents move beyond chat interfaces into physical AI systems, where software requests can trigger devices, robots, payments, and real-world workflows.

The local `agent-q` host process relays signing requests. Agent-Q Firmware runs on a separate device and decides whether signing is authorized through a device-owned policy gate or a device-local approval gate.

The host is transport, not authority.

A requester cannot:

- hold signing material;

- choose the authorization mode;

- directly commit policy changes;

- approve its own request.

Once a request enters the Agent-Q signing path, the authorization gate is not optional. The requester cannot choose that gate; Firmware derives it from device-local state. Every supported signing request must pass through that gate before a signature is produced.

**Why this boundary matters**

Most agent wallets start with familiar policies: budget, expiry, revocation, allowlists, and protocol scope.

Those are useful primitives. But the hard question is not what the policy says.

The hard question is what the requester must prove before signing.

In physical AI, the requester may not be a browser wallet or a human-operated app. It may be a robot, local automation process, MCP client, or embedded system acting from sensor input and model output.

That makes the signing boundary more important, not less.

The system that requests action should not automatically become the system that authorizes value movement or protocol execution.

On-chain policy objects are useful inside contract paths that opt into them. They can work well for vaults, escrow flows, payment modules, and protocol-specific control paths.

Agent signing is broader than one contract path. A requester can assemble different PTB shapes, call different packages, use different inputs, or interact with protocols that were not designed around the same policy object.

Agent-Q works at the signing boundary for that reason. It does not require every protocol to become policy-aware or route through a single super-contract. Instead, every supported request must expose enough facts for the device to evaluate before a signature is produced.

If the requester hides required facts, uses an unsupported transaction shape, or avoids the policy path, policy mode fails closed instead of trusting the host, UI, RPC, dry-run, or agent explanation.

Agent-Q does not try to make the agent trustworthy.

Agent-Q makes the request accountable to a device-owned authority boundary.

**Why Sui**

Agent-Q is Sui-native because it uses Sui’s transaction model as the policy substrate.

Sui is not used as a payment rail bolted onto an agent. Agent-Q uses Sui transaction structure to decide what may be signed.

Sui Programmable Transaction Blocks give signing requests a structured executable shape. Agent-Q does not rely on explanations. It checks supported facts from the request context and signable transaction bytes.

Those facts can include:

- request network;

- account binding;

- command structure;

- Move call target;

- input kind;

- type tag;

- recipient field;

- transfer structure;

- balance source;

- policy-relevant token facts.

The important point is not that Agent-Q has a long list of policies.

The important point is that supported policies are tied to transaction facts the device can verify before signing.

Agent-Q does not treat arbitrary PTBs as safe. It evaluates supported transaction shapes. Unsupported or incomplete shapes fail closed in policy mode.

**Address Balances and FundsWithdrawal<T>**

Sui Address Balances and `FundsWithdrawal<T>`-style PTB inputs are an important Sui-specific advantage for Agent-Q.

A transaction that withdraws from an address balance can carry asset type, amount, and balance source inside the signable transaction input.

That means the device can verify offline, from PTB/request facts:

- what token type is being withdrawn;

- how much is being withdrawn;

- whether the balance source is sender or sponsor.

For a token-spending policy to pass offline, the requester must use a Sui transaction shape that exposes the required facts to the device.

For token type, amount, and balance-source policies, the preferred path is an Address Balances / `FundsWithdrawal<T>`-style PTB input.

If the requester uses an object-only coin input, or another shape where the device cannot determine the required token facts, policy mode fails closed.

Agent-Q requires the request to be shaped in a way the device can verify.

**Policy patterns**

Agent-Q policies are not just daily caps or address lists.

The goal is to restrict execution target and asset movement together.

Examples:

- a treasury workflow may use only specific token types and balance sources;

- a deployment workflow may call only approved package / module / function targets;

- a procurement workflow may withdraw only from the sender balance and only within a defined amount;

- a payment flow may bind asset type, amount, recipient, and expected destination together;

- a physical AI workflow may let a robot or embedded system request execution while keeping value movement and protocol calls outside the requester’s authority;

- an automation system may reject an unknown Move call target even if the amount is small;

- a policy update may be proposed by a requester, but committed only through the device-owned approval gate;

- unsupported or incomplete transaction shapes must fail closed.

These are transaction-shape requirements.

Agent-Q verifies the Sui request facts it can parse and signs only when the current policy allows it.

In user authorization mode, the device can show covered offline facts where available. Otherwise, a valid account-bound request is treated as blind signing and must be explicitly confirmed through the device-local approval path.

**What is implemented**

The current implementation includes:

- Sui CLI external signer support through `agent-q-sui-signer`;

- MCP tools for agent-driven signing requests;

- supported Sui Wallet Standard provider flows;

- bounded Sui transaction signing paths;

- bounded personal-message signing paths;

- offline policy facts for supported Sui transaction shapes;

- token totals by type for policy evaluation;

- policy proposal handling through device-owned approval gates;

- non-production Sui zkLogin identity and signing paths: browser-to-device proof setup, bounded proof storage, active account projection, device-local proof clear, and authorization-gated zkLogin signature-envelope construction;

- explicit unsupported-method failures.

Policy updates are also requests, not host-side setters.

A requester may submit bounded policy material, but Firmware validates it and commits it only after the implemented device-local approval path.

**Current stage**

The current StackChan CoreS3 Firmware path is configured for development and demos, not real-asset custody.

This is a custody-readiness boundary, not a change in the core architecture. The current submission focuses on the device-owned signing boundary, Sui-native policy enforcement, and pre-signing verification based on request facts.

Production custody would require a production material profile, secure material storage, Secure Boot, Flash Encryption, eFuse-based provisioning, signed firmware update management, rollback protection, and managed release channels.

**Future direction: identity-bound delegation**

zkLogin is not signing authority by itself.

In Agent-Q’s long-term model, zkLogin can connect a human-controlled, OAuth-backed identity to Sui as a delegation anchor. It gives a human principal a Sui-native way to connect off-chain identity to delegated on-chain action without giving the requester the human’s account, keys, custody, or signing authority.

In that model, the requester does not become the human. The requester receives an identity-bound delegation and bounded execution scope.

That delegation gives AI action a source and a limit: who authorized it, what it may do, what token limits apply, which methods or Move call targets are allowed, what approval rules remain, and how the delegation can be revoked.

Agent-Q’s direction is to enforce that identity-bound scope before signing: richer offline policy facts, more reviewable PTB shapes, stricter role scope, token limits, Move call allowlists, balance-source rules, output checks, and delegated identity paths.

The goal is not to make AI, robots, or automation systems more powerful by giving them keys.

The goal is to let humans give those systems a bounded identity for action while keeping signing authority outside the requester runtime.

A registered agent is not automatically an authorized agent.

Authorization starts with delegation.

Anyone can request. Only the device decides whether signing is authorized.

About:

**Sprout**

An intent execution engine for Sui. Describe an outcome in plain English and Sprout turns it into a single atomic, fully sponsored on-chain transaction that you review in full before signing — and records an agent-signed, on-chain-verified receipt of what it did.

**Overview**

Sprout executes DeFi rather than describing it. You state an outcome, for example "swap everything to USDC and spread it across the safest three vaults" or "convert half my SUI and send it to alice.sui", and Sprout works out every action that outcome requires (swaps, splits, merges, vault deposits, withdrawals, transfers) and composes them into one atomic transaction on Sui. Whether the request is complex or trivial, it resolves to a single signature. Every step settles together or none of them do.

Moving funds is the straightforward part. The harder part is doing it in a way the user can trust, so Sprout pairs execution with three guarantees: an Adaptive Risk Guardian that flags every relevant risk, a PTB viewer that presents the exact transaction in plain language, and a strict rule that nothing reaches the chain without the user reading and signing it. Every transaction is also fully sponsored, so no gas, dust, or SUI balance is required to act.

Plain-English intent goes in; a reviewed, atomic, gas-free transaction comes out.

**Core capabilities**

*Any Sui action, composed into one transaction.*

Sprout supports swaps, splits, merges, multi-vault deposits, withdrawals, and transfers, and composes them in parallel inside a single transaction. A request like "swap everything to USDC and spread it across the top three vaults" becomes one atomic operation. Every step succeeds together or the whole transaction reverts, so there are no partial states and no sequence of wallet prompts to manage.

*An Adaptive Risk Guardian.*

The Guardian reviews each transaction and surfaces every meaningful risk before the user commits. It is adaptive: as a transaction grows more complex, the review expands to cover it point by point. Capacity limits, fees, reward dependence, real-world-asset and lockup caveats, beta status, and irreversible transfers are each stated in plain language the user can read in one pass.

*A PTB viewer.*

Before signing, Sprout shows the actual Programmable Transaction Block as a readable summary and a clear step-by-step flow: what moves, where it goes, and why. The user sees exactly what will be submitted to the chain instead of raw transaction data.

*Full gas sponsorship.*

Every action and transfer run through Sprout is sponsored. Users do not need to hold SUI, top up gas, or keep dust on hand to transact.

*Verifiable on-chain receipts.*

Every action and payment can emit an agent-signed `SproutReceipt` / `SproutPayReceipt` event whose signature the on-chain contract verifies before recording it — a tamper-evident, queryable trail of what the agent did (a plain-English summary, the risk shown to the user, and a deterministic action list). The receipt rides inside the same atomic transaction as the action it describes and can never block it.

*Memory that stays yours.*

With your sign-off, Sprout remembers what matters to you — your risk tolerance, the tokens and strategies you favor, the goals you are working toward — and recalls them in later sessions, so you never have to re-explain yourself. Facts are distilled from the conversation, encrypted, and stored on Walrus, scoped to your wallet on Sui. Turning memory on takes a single signature proving the wallet is yours, so only you can read or clear it, and one click forgets everything. It never blocks the conversation: recall is best-effort and the save happens after the reply.

**The core principle**

Sprout never takes an action the user did not ask for, and the user always sees the full transaction before signing.

An agent that handles funds is only safe if a person remains the final approver. Every action, without exception, is presented in full through the PTB viewer and the Guardian's risk review before a signature is requested. If the agent gets something wrong, the user catches it on screen, because there is no path to the chain that bypasses review. There are no blind confirmations and no silent execution. Nothing leaves the wallet until the user has read, understood, and signed it, and the agent cannot exceed the permissions it was given.

**Built on Sui**

Sprout relies on several Sui primitives that make this approach possible:

- **Programmable Transaction Blocks (PTBs)** allow a full multi-step plan to execute atomically under a single signature.

- **Sponsored transactions (Enoki) **let Sprout cover gas, so users do not need SUI to get started.

- **SuiNS** resolves names like `alice.sui`, so recipients can be confirmed by name.

- **Object-centric composability** lets Sprout route across protocols, using the 7K aggregator for swaps and Ember Finance for vaults — no custom contract for the money movement itself.

- **A custom **`sprout::receipt`** Move package** verifies the agent's ed25519 signature on-chain and emits the receipt events, so every attestation is provably Sprout's and cannot be spoofed.

- **Walrus + Seal (memwal)** give the agent encrypted, wallet-scoped memory: facts are stored as encrypted blobs on Walrus, and a wallet signature proving ownership gates every read and write, so the memory is portable and provably the user's.

A generic language model that happens to hold SUI cannot do any of this. The agent is more capable, safer, and more composable because it runs on Sui.

**How it works**

- The user states a goal.

- Sprout reasons through it, calls on-chain tools to fetch live quotes and vault data, and assembles a plan.

- The plan renders as a PTB viewer card alongside the Guardian's risk review, showing exactly what will be submitted and why.

- The user reviews and signs once. The plan executes atomically and Sprout sponsors the gas.

**Additional features**

- **On-chain reads.** Balances, vault positions and yield, recent activity for any address, token market data, and SuiNS lookups.

- **Explanations on demand. **Ask about a concept such as impermanent loss and Sprout explains it in the context of what is on screen.

About:

**Your AI has the context. Your wallet has the authority.**
*Delegate the work, not the wallet. See the transaction before you sign it.*

On-chain actions usually pull you out of the assistant you were already using — into a separate app, a new tab, and a context-blind wallet flow. Say Ur Intent brings Sui transaction review into the AI client you already use: tell an AI what you want to do on Sui, read the transaction as a local PTB review, and sign in your own wallet — without breaking the thread, and without ever handing the AI your keys.

In most on-chain AI systems, a smarter agent becomes a riskier one. Here, a smarter agent just does better work — it still can't hold your keys, sign for you, or act on its own.

`The risky pattern:   AI ─ keys or signing authority ─▶ acts on its own
Say Ur Intent:       AI ─ local review evidence ──▶ you approve in your wallet`
**Why Sui**

Say Ur Intent uses Sui's Programmable Transaction Block (PTB) model as the review surface. A PTB makes the transaction structure explicit before signing: the command sequence, input references, object arguments, and call arguments are part of the transaction material itself — not hidden behind a single opaque call. That does not make every Move call semantically transparent; what those calls do still depends on on-chain state at execution. But it gives Say Ur Intent a concrete structure to build, check, visualize, and bind before wallet handoff.

A Sui transaction is not treated as a black-box payload. Say Ur Intent turns the supported transaction material into a graph of inputs, Move calls, coin operations, transfers, shared objects, and protocol calls. The graph is not a mock preview; it is derived from the same transaction material whose digest is checked before wallet handoff.

The reviewed material is bound to a Sui transaction digest, and bytes reach your wallet only after the stored material recomputes to the reviewed commitment:

`what you reviewed == what your wallet receives`
That guarantee is byte-level: the bytes handed to the wallet are the bytes bound to the reviewed commitment — not a claim that the human-readable review captures every detail of the transaction. The raw PTB structure and addresses stay inspectable beside it.

**Why it's safer**

The AI never gets signing power — MCP responses carry no signatures, no signing requests, and no executable bytes, and signing happens only from the local review page, in your own wallet.

Say Ur Intent builds the transaction, not the AI. It builds or verifies the material locally against pinned mainnet packages and objects, and checks it with quote policy, object-ownership, and review-time simulation. It does not blindly forward arbitrary PTBs. New actions are added through explicit review adapters that define what can be built, checked, visualized, and handed to the wallet. Unsupported material fails closed instead of being forwarded to wallet review.

**Your model, your tools**

Say Ur Intent is not a hosted AI DeFi app with a model you did not choose — it's the open-source MCP server you run with the client and model you already trust: the latest model, or the one you've settled on. And because it's MCP-based, it composes with the other tools in that same client — security checks, wallet context, monitoring, protocol data, or future agent workflows — around the same local review flow, instead of being locked inside one app.

**What's real today**

Say Ur Intent is a mainnet-only Sui intent and review layer. Today you can take a supported plain-English swap on DeepBook or FlowX all the way through:

`intent → local review evidence → PTB visualization → digest-gated wallet handoff → user-controlled wallet signing → execution receipt recording`
The review evidence can include quote policy, object-ownership checks, a human-readable transaction review, and review-time simulation, rendered alongside a PTB visualization of the transaction. Review-time simulation is evidence at review time, not a guarantee of the final on-chain result.

It also answers supported Sui asset and DeFi evidence questions and creates local review sessions. Transaction bytes move only through the digest-gated wallet handoff initiated from the local review page, and the signing decision stays with you.

**What it doesn't do**

Say Ur Intent doesn't custody funds, hold private keys, trade autonomously, rank venues, pick "best" routes, silently choose a settlement token, cash out to fiat, compute P&L, or promise a USDC/USD peg.

It isn't an AI trader, and it isn't your advisor — the reasoning stays with your agent, the signing stays with you, and it's the local review layer in between that keeps AI help separate from wallet authority.

**A smarter agent, not a riskier one**

Because the AI never gets signing authority, a better model improves the work, not the risk. Over time Say Ur Intent grows three ways: more supported actions through explicit review adapters, more review checks before approval, and better AI/MCP assistance around the same local review path.

Because Say Ur Intent is open source, the review adapter contract is also an extension point. A Sui protocol can contribute a review adapter by PR — not as a blind SDK drop-in: each adapter must clear the safety bar by building or verifying transaction material against pinned packages, deterministic checks, and a reviewable PTB. Every accepted adapter adds one more action users can review and sign through the same local path. Protocols get a vetted pre-sign review surface; users get broader coverage without giving the AI more authority.

It gets more capable without ever giving the AI control of your wallet.

**Links**

- GitHub: [https://github.com/stelis-dev/say-ur-intent](https://github.com/stelis-dev/say-ur-intent)

- NPM: [https://www.npmjs.com/package/@stelis/say-ur-intent](https://www.npmjs.com/package/@stelis/say-ur-intent)

- MCP Registry: [https://registry.modelcontextprotocol.io/v0/servers?search=say-ur-intent](https://registry.modelcontextprotocol.io/v0/servers?search=say-ur-intent)

About:

**Cartesian DemoForge is an AI-agent pipeline for proof-first website modernization.**

It helps discover small businesses with outdated or weak websites, audits their problems, explains those problems visually, and prepares a live mobile-first demo direction before the customer pays.

Most local businesses do not need another abstract website proposal. They need to see what a better website could actually look like for their business. Cartesian DemoForge turns this idea into a repeatable agentic workflow: find the business, analyze the current website, identify concrete problems, classify them into clear business categories, prepare improved content, generate a demo direction, and support human-reviewed outreach.

**The core principle is simple:**

Do not only tell the customer that their website can be improved. Show the customer a working preview.

**The problem**

Small businesses often lose trust, phone calls, bookings, and local search visibility because their websites are slow, outdated, difficult to use on mobile, hard to contact, or poorly optimized for local search.

Many business owners do not see these issues as technical problems. They only see fewer leads, weaker trust, and fewer customer inquiries.

**Cartesian DemoForge translates website weaknesses into clear business problems:**

- Performance: slow loading, poor responsiveness, weak user experience.

- Mobile: broken layouts, unreadable sections, or uncomfortable navigation on phones.

- Security and trust: outdated presentation, weak trust signals, missing or unclear security indicators.

- Conversion: missing click-to-call, weak contact flow, unclear call to action.

- Local visibility: weak map/search readiness, poor local SEO, unclear service-area messaging.

**The solution**

Cartesian DemoForge uses specialized AI agents to automate the early website modernization workflow while keeping human review in control.

**The pipeline includes:**

- ***Scout Agent***

Finds small businesses with outdated, weak, or missing web presence.

- ***Auditor Agent***

Analyzes the current website and detects performance, mobile, trust, SEO, and conversion problems.

- ***Classifier Agent***

Maps each detected issue to a customer-facing problem category and selects the correct visual diagnosis card.

- ***Content Agent***

Prepares improved website copy, service positioning, section structure, and customer-facing messaging.

- ***Builder Agent***

Creates a mobile-first demo direction based on the audit, business category, and detected problems.

- ***Visual Agent***

Prepares problem-specific visual cards and before/after presentation assets.

- ***Outreach Agent***

Drafts personalized customer messages based on the audit findings and demo direction.

- ***Orchestrator***

Coordinates the full workflow, keeps the process structured, and ensures human review before anything is sent to a customer.

**What makes it different**

Cartesian DemoForge is not a generic website generator and not a traditional website agency workflow.

It is an agentic production system for local business modernization.

Traditional agencies usually start with a pitch. Cartesian DemoForge starts with proof.

The system combines lead discovery, website audit, problem classification, visual diagnosis, demo generation, and personalized outreach into one repeatable workflow. AI agents accelerate the production process, but the final delivery remains human-reviewed, controlled, and business-safe.

**Current proof-of-concept**

The current prototype focuses on local service businesses such as roofing, construction, repair, cleaning, dental, beauty, and other categories where website trust directly affects customer conversion.

**The prototype demonstrates:**

- real local business examples,

- audit-based issue classification,

- visual problem cards,

- mobile-first improvement direction,

- customer-facing explanations,

- outreach-ready positioning,

- human-reviewed delivery flow.

**Example diagnosis categories include:**

- Performance cards: speed, loading, responsiveness.

- Mobile cards: smartphone layout and mobile usability.

- Security cards: trust, lock, shield, SSL/security perception.

- Conversion cards: phone, click-to-call, calendar, booking path.

- Local SEO cards: map pin, search visibility, ranking, local discovery.

**Tech stack**

- Next.js / TypeScript frontend.

- Vercel deployment for public web delivery, preview builds, and fast iteration.

- AWS cloud infrastructure direction for production hosting, storage, automation, and scalable services.

- PostgreSQL / SQL database layer for structured business data, audit results, lead records, and workflow state.

- AI-assisted audit workflow for detecting website problems and modernization opportunities.

- Deterministic multi-agent pipeline design for repeatable lead discovery, audit, demo, and outreach workflows.

- Issue classification system for mapping detected problems into business-friendly categories.

- Card-based visual diagnosis system for performance, mobile, security, conversion, and local SEO problems.

- Human-reviewed customer delivery model to keep final outreach controlled, safe, and business-ready.

- GitHub-based development workflow for source control, iteration, and release tracking.

**Why now**

Small businesses are surrounded by AI products, but most of them do not need abstract AI automation. They need visible business improvement.

Cartesian DemoForge applies AI agents to a practical, high-friction business workflow: finding weak websites, explaining what is wrong, preparing a better version, and helping the owner understand the value before paying.

This makes the sales process more concrete, more visual, and more useful for the customer.

**Vision**

The long-term vision is to build an AI-assisted modernization engine for local businesses: a system that can discover weak websites, audit them, generate live demos, prepare outreach, and support small agencies or solo operators with a repeatable production workflow.

Cartesian DemoForge is the first step toward that system.

**Final summary**

Cartesian DemoForge helps small businesses see their future website before they buy it.

About:

**AstraDesk Command Core is a human-supervised AI mission-control layer for AeroNerve 2.0.**

It connects human operator intent with specialized AI agents that assist with mission context analysis, operational reasoning, task coordination, support workflows, and controlled platform actions.

AstraDesk is not just a dashboard and not just a chatbot. It is a command interface for agent-driven operational workflows, designed around human oversight, auditability, modularity, and secure enterprise-grade execution.

**The problem**

Modern drone operations are becoming increasingly complex. Operators may need to monitor telemetry, reason about mission context, coordinate multiple units, inspect risks, respond to incidents, and keep a clear record of what happened and why.

A normal dashboard can show data, but it does not actively help the operator reason through the situation.

At the same time, fully autonomous AI control is not acceptable for mission-oriented workflows. Operators need AI assistance, but they also need authority, review, traceability, and clear safety boundaries.

AstraDesk Command Core is built around one principle:

AI agents can assist, analyze, explain, and prepare actions.
The human operator remains in control.

**The solution**

AstraDesk Command Core adds an agentic orchestration layer to the AeroNerve 2.0 command-and-control workflow.

It supports:

- mission context analysis,

- telemetry interpretation,

- operational reasoning,

- multi-drone coordination,

- support and incident workflows,

- controlled platform actions,

- audit-ready explanations,

- human-reviewed decision support.

**Agentic architecture**

- ***Operator Interface***

The human operator gives commands, asks questions, reviews suggestions, and approves controlled actions.

- ***Context Agent***

Analyzes mission context, platform state, telemetry, and operational inputs.

- ***Reasoning Agent***

Transforms raw context into structured recommendations, risk notes, and next-step proposals.

- ***Coordination Agent***

Helps organize tasks across multiple drones, mission phases, or operational units.

- ***Support Agent***

Assists with incident explanation, procedural guidance, and operator-facing documentation.

- ***Action Agent***

Prepares controlled platform actions that remain policy-aware and human-reviewed.

- ***Audit Layer***

Records what was requested, what the agent recommended, what the operator approved, and what action was taken.

- ***Command Orchestrator***

Coordinates the agents, routes requests, applies policy boundaries, and keeps the workflow structured and reviewable.

**What makes it different**

AstraDesk Command Core is not an autonomous black-box drone brain.

It is a human-supervised agentic mission-control layer.

The system is designed to reduce operator cognitive load without removing operator authority. AI agents help the operator understand the mission, reason about the situation, coordinate tasks, and prepare actions — but the human remains responsible for final decisions.

**Current proof-of-concept**

The current prototype demonstrates the command-core direction for AeroNerve 2.0 and shows how AstraDesk can act as an AI orchestration layer for operational systems.

The project includes:

- AstraDesk AI agent framework foundation,

- AeroNerve 2.0 platform integration direction,

- simulator-oriented mission-control narrative,

- human-in-the-loop command concept,

- modular agent workflow design,

- public project website,

- public GitHub repository,

- enterprise-oriented auditability and governance direction.

[*AeroNerve 2.0*](https://aeronerve.co)** platform context**

AeroNerve 2.0 focuses on reliable drone operations, simulation, command and control, observability, secure communications, and future AI-assisted mission workflows.

AstraDesk Command Core fits into this platform as the AI command and operator-assistance layer.

Tech stack direction

- AstraDesk AI agent framework

- Python / FastAPI agent gateway

- Java / Spring Boot service integration layer

- Next.js / TypeScript operator interface

- PostgreSQL / pgvector for structured memory and RAG

- Redis for working memory

- NATS for event-driven communication

- OpenTelemetry, Prometheus, Grafana, Loki, and Tempo for observability

- OIDC/JWT, RBAC, mTLS, and audit trails for enterprise security

**Why now**

AI agents are moving from simple chat interfaces into operational workflows. But in mission-oriented systems, the key challenge is not just automation. The real challenge is controlled assistance.

AstraDesk Command Core explores this direction: an agentic command layer where AI helps the operator understand and manage complex drone operations while preserving safety, traceability, and human decision authority.

**Final summary**

AstraDesk Command Core turns AeroNerve 2.0 into an agent-assisted mission-control environment where human operators can coordinate drone workflows with AI support, controlled actions, and complete auditability.

**SUI Intent Engine** — a Sui-native intent engine for the Agentic Web. You describe the trade in plain English ("buy 1 SUI if RSI < 30, 2% slip"), the engine compiles it into a PTB, runs a 6-dimension Guardian

  risk check, and shows you a human-readable preview. You confirm, your wallet signs, DeepBook V3 executes on Sui mainnet.

  **The flow** — PARSE → GUARD → PREVIEW → SIGN. No black boxes. The AI proposes, the indicators veto, the human decides.

  **What's different** — the Guardian layer. Six checks (RSI, MACD, Bollinger, KDJ, Volume, ADX) surface slippage, weak trends, and low liquidity in plain language *before* you sign. Plus a multi-timeframe AI Quant

  engine (5m / 15m / 1h / 4h consensus) with confidence-scored entries, take-profit, and stop-loss.

  **Stack** — React + TypeScript + dapp-kit, DeepBook V3, Cetus BM indexer, MiniMax LLM (with <think> strip). Deployed on Sui mainnet, verified on SuiVision, bilingual EN/ZH.

About:

### Tako - Move-Native Policy Wallet for Autonomous Trading Agents

Tako is a Move-native policy wallet for autonomous trading agents.

A user signs a `SpendPolicy` once. Every DeepBook fill routes through `settlement::execute_trade_direct`. Any violation aborts the entire PTB.

The headline demo is not a server log: it is a live on-chain rejection counter (`cap_rejections`) that increases in real time, plus a leaked-key red-team scenario

where pausing the parent policy freezes every child policy in a single transaction.

### 1. The Problem

Every autonomous trading agent today asks users to trust operator logs or platform runtime limits.

Neither is enforceable in code.

EVM `approve` is a leaky bucket:

- No per-trade scope

- No expiry

- No proof of compliance

Solana programs require bespoke per-application delegation.

Brief and Lyra-class policy wallets on DeepSurge ship mainnet USDC fills, but none combine:

- DeepBook depth proof

- Live attack-feed KPI

- Sub-delegation double-spend protection

- 24/7 supervised agent operation

on testnet in a single system.

### 2. What We Built

Tako never holds funds.

Authorization delegates through DeepBook's audited `BalanceManager` + `TradeCap` model.

The settlement module is the only path into DeepBook V3 spot.

Six policy dimensions are enforced atomically in Move:

- Maximum quantity

- Daily base cap

- Daily quote budget

- Slippage floor

- Pool allowlist

- TTL (time-to-live)

Additional guarantees:

- One-transaction revocation via `PolicyCap`

- Append-only shared `ActivityLog`

- Per-fill `TradeProofReceipt` events

### Advanced Layers Shipped and Verified

- **Leaked-key cascade**

- Parent `set_paused(true)` blocks every child via `EParentRevoked` (code 16)

- **Sub-delegation tree**

- v6 closes shared-BalanceManager double-spend through atomic `record_child_fill` on parent

- **Grandchild bypass prevention**

- v7 limits delegation depth to one hop from root

- **Recipient allowlist**

- `ActionReceipt` support for non-DeepBook payout paths

- Defends against prompt-injection payout attacks

- **Walrus session replay**

- MemTrace adapter enables cross-session resume

- **Nautilus TEE attestation**

- Real Ed25519 verification over SHA-384 measurements

- Hardware deployment on Nitro EC2 honestly marked as pending

### 3. Architecture

`User Wallet
    │
    ├── creates ──► SpendPolicy (shared)
    │               PolicyCap (owned)
    │
Agent Relay
    │
    └── holds ───► TradeCap
                    (delegated from BalanceManager)
                         │
                         ▼
 settlement::execute_trade_direct
                         │
                         ▼
 assert_order_permitted
                         │
                         ▼
 DeepBook place_market_order
                         │
                         ▼
 ActivityLog.count++
 TradeProofReceipt event
`
### Delegation Model

Sub-policies are attenuated children:

- Stricter caps

- Subset of pools

- Reduced authority

Parent policies aggregate child fills so that multiple children cannot collectively exceed the parent epoch budget.

Attack probes call `record_rejection`, which re-validates policy constraints before incrementing `cap_rejections`.

### 4. On-Chain Proof

- Includes sub-delegation negative tests

- Includes limit-order quote-budget charging tests

### 6. Why We Beat Brief, Lyra, and Mandate Memory

### 7. What Runs 24/7

### 8. Tech Stack

### Smart Contracts

- Move

- `spend_policy`

- `enclave_attestation`

### Infrastructure

### 9. Judge Verification Checklist

### No credentials required.

- Confirm production URL returns HTTP 200.

- Open ActivityLog object on Suiscan and verify `count` matches dashboard fill count.

- Verify attack-policy `cap_rejections` is monotonic over a five-minute window.

- Replay digest `2WXt76Ud` and confirm:

- `MoveAbort`

- `spend_policy::assert_order_permitted`

- Replay digest `4FSVGjhx` and confirm:

- `TradeProofReceipt`

- Counterparty is not the agent BalanceManager.

- Run:

`sui move test
`inside:

`provenant/move
`Expected result:

`44 / 44 passing
`
- Optional:

- Inject over-cap trade

- Verify identical abort code on-chain

### 10. Composability on Sui

`SpendPolicy` is a shared object that any PTB can reference.

###

### Key Features

### 1. Atomic Policy Enforcement

Six-axis `SpendPolicy` enforced atomically in Move with one-transaction `PolicyCap` revocation.

### 2. Non-Custodial Architecture

DeepBook-native `TradeCap` delegation means Tako never custodies user funds.

### 3. Live Security KPI

Real-time `cap_rejections` metric powered by `CapRejection` events.

### 4. Verified Trading Activity

50 verified fills recorded in a shared `ActivityLog`.

### 5. Delegatable Security

Attenuating child policies with cascade revocation via `EParentRevoked` (code 16).

### 6. Policy-Gated Orders

Policy-enforced limit orders and cancels proven on-chain.

### 7. Explainable Trading

Walrus-anchored LLM trade rationale with MemTrace session replay support.

### 8. Safe Payout Controls

Recipient allowlists and per-action `ActionReceipt` protection for non-DeepBook payouts.

### 9. Preflight Simulation

`devInspect` dry-run validation before signing.

### 10. TEE Verification

Real Ed25519 enclave attestation verification on-chain (Route B), with Nitro hardware deployment explicitly deferred.

About:

The **AEGIS Project **describes a Move-native "execution firewall" built for the Sui blockchain. Below is a detailed breakdown of the system components based on the provided document.

### **1. Core Concept & North Star**

Aegis acts as a safety primitive for autonomous agents on the blockchain. Instead of an agent interacting directly with protocols, Aegis wraps the agent's intended action within an `AegisIntent` object. This object acts as a "smart" container that enforces risk-gated execution, maintains a persistent memory of the intent's lifecycle, and provides human/DAO override capabilities.

### **2. System Architecture Layers**

The system is divided into four distinct layers:

- **Layer 1 (External Data):** The agent consumes real-time data from sources like DeepBook (price/liquidity feeds) and the Sui Clock object (for verifiable timestamps).

- **Layer 2 (Agent - TypeScript):** The off-chain agent monitors the `AegisRegistry` for new intents, runs a multi-factor risk engine, and triggers on-chain Programmable Transaction Blocks (PTBs) once safety thresholds are met.

- **Layer 3 (Sui Blockchain):** The core on-chain layer containing the `AegisIntent` (owned object) and its associated `Dynamic Fields` that store state (risk, execution logs, policy rules).

- **Layer 4 (Frontend):** A React-based interface allowing users to view intent graphs, monitor risk timelines, and trigger the "kill switch" for emergency revocations.

### **3. Sui Primitives Implementation**

Aegis heavily utilizes unique Sui-specific primitives to ensure the system is performant and verifiable:

- `AegisIntent`** (Owned Objects):** Because these are owned objects rather than entries in a global mapping, they scale horizontally; 1,000 agents can operate simultaneously without contending for a single global state.

- **Dynamic Fields ("The Brain"):** Rather than using rigid, fixed structs, Aegis uses Dynamic Fields to store `risk`, `log`, `notes`, `market`, and `policy` data. This allows the system to add new categories of data (like PQC metadata) via upgrades without breaking existing objects.

- **Programmable Transaction Blocks (PTBs):** Execution is atomic. For example, the `execute_intent` PTB ensures that a risk check, the actual DeepBook swap, and the execution log update all happen in one transaction. If the risk check fails, the transaction aborts and no funds are moved.

### **4. Security & Authorization**

Aegis uses a robust capability-based security model:

- `AegisCapability`**:** Agents possess this object to call `update_risk` and `execute_intent`.

- **Kill Switch:** Creators (DAOs/users) retain unilateral revocation power to set an intent status to `REVOKED`, immediately rendering it inexecutable.

- **Attack Mitigation:** The system limits the rate of risk score changes to prevent rogue agents from "gaming" the score, and uses "hot potato" `ApprovalTicket` patterns to prevent replay attacks.

### **5. Quantum Readiness (PQC)**

While the project does not involve quantum computing for risk scoring, it incorporates "cryptographic agility" to prepare for future quantum threats (e.g., Shor’s Algorithm breaking Ed25519):

- `quantum_status`** field:** A tag on the intent object that enables future-proofing.

- **Classical Heuristics:** The `RiskEngine` includes a `quantum_risk` factor that penalizes long-lived, high-value treasury intents, treating them as more exposed to "store now, decrypt later" attacks.

- **Alignment:** The system follows the migration path outlined in the *Post-Quantum Readiness in EdDSA Chains* (FC 2026) paper, allowing for future ZK-proof seed migration without changing addresses.

About:

SuiPump is a permissionless bonding-curve token launchpad on Sui with an autonomous on-chain trading agent at its core. The agent executes a full token lifecycle — launch, dev-buy, monitor, and claim fees — as a verifiable Talus Nexus DAG, acting on Sui with no human in the loop.

**The autonomous agent:**

- A Talus Nexus agent runs a directed acyclic graph of on-chain actions: launch a token → execute a dev-buy → monitor the curve → claim creator fees, with outputs of each step wired into the next.

- Five signed-HTTP tool servers (launch, buy, sell, claim, alerts), written in Rust, expose SuiPump's contract calls to the agent. Every action is a real Sui transaction, settled on-chain.

- The agent operates against a committed sub-balance, so it transacts autonomously within a scoped mandate the wallet authorizes once — no standing custody of user funds.

- Verified end-to-end on testnet: autonomous launch + buy executed and confirmed on-chain.

**The underlying protocol:**

- Anyone launches a fungible token in 2 wallet signatures, no code. Price discovery runs through an on-chain constant-product AMM.

- **Oracle-based dynamic graduation** — the graduation threshold is recomputed on every buy against SUI's live USD price (Pyth), so every token graduates at a consistent dollar market cap. Tokens graduate to Cetus, Turbos, or DeepBook.

- 1% trade fee with 40% to creators (vs [pump.fun](http://pump.fun)'s 24%); 10% LP fee retained in the curve.

- An AI risk-analysis layer scores each token from live on-chain holder concentration and trading data.

**Current state:** Live on Sui testnet, stress-tested to 658,000+ SUI in volume. 55/55 Move unit tests + 59/59 Python harness passing. Agent tooling registered and verified on testnet. Internal pre-audit review complete with zero critical, high, or medium-severity findings. Targeting mainnet after an independent third-party Move audit.

**Tech stack:** Move smart contracts (fully on-chain, non-custodial) + Talus Nexus agent with Rust signed-HTTP tool servers + React/Vite frontend with @mysten/dapp-kit. A lightweight off-chain indexer provides read-only data; it holds no funds and sits in no transaction path.

About:

Vyom is a sui aligned sealed capsule platform for creating time locked messages that can be shared by link and optionally revealed only by a specific Sui wallet.

Users can create a capsule, define when it unlocks, choose between link access, wallet gated access and share the direct capsule link. Before the unlock time, message remains sealed. After unlock, the capsule can either reveal through link access or require recipient wallet verification through the required sui wallet.

Current release includes time based unlocks, shareable capsule links, sui wallet gated reveal, wallet owned vault, wallet connect/disconnect for identity, and a static demo capsule experience.

Vyom is currently focused on sealed message capsules. Our future work includes client side encryption, signature based unlock verification, media capsules, one time reveal, NFT based gating, and richer programmable unlock conditions.

(Note: A minimal Vyom registry package is deployed on Sui Testnet as an onchain project marker for the current release.)

Media:

About:

### **Sup Wallet — The Self-Custodial Wallet for the Agent Economy**

**Hire an agent. Give it an allowance. Strategize your assets.
Sup is a self-custodial wallet on Sui. It lets an AI agent research, trade, pay, and settle on its own, but only inside limits you set once. The agent never holds your private key. Instead of signing each transaction yourself, you grant the agent an allowance.

### **TL;DR**

The hard part of letting agents handle money isn’t making them smart. It’s trusting them with funds. Give an agent your keys and a single prompt injection can drain your wallet. Make it ask for a signature every time and it can’t act on its own. Today every agent hits the same wall: it needs a human to approve each move.
Sup adds the piece that’s missing: a budget that’s capped, scoped to specific protocols, and revocable, all enforced by a Move object on-chain. You give the agent a ceiling and a sandbox, say up to 500 USDC and DeepBook only. You tell it your goal in plain language. It then researches the task, builds a Sui transaction, shows you the risks, and runs within its limits. Every action writes to an on-chain log you can review, and you can shut it off with one click.
Those protocols plug in as adapters, where each adapter is one skill the agent can run, such as a Cetus swap or a DeepBook order. The set of adapters is open, so anyone can publish a new skill and the agent’s reach grows with the marketplace.

### **1. The Problem: There’s No Spending Boundary**

Wallets today give you two bad options and nothing in between.

- The approve wall. Every swap, loan, or order needs a human signature, so the agent can’t run on its own.

- All-or-nothing approvals. One approval is unbounded, which leaves the whole vault exposed.

Keys equal permission. There’s no way to say “this much, here only, and I can take it back.” A signature works like a password, not like proof of what’s allowed.

### **2. The Core Idea: Revocable Delegation as a Move Object**

Sup’s delegation lives on Sui as a Move object. It separates two questions that wallets usually merge: who is allowed to spend, and whether a given spend is allowed. Each question gets its own on-chain gate.

`Owner's Vault (self-custodial Move object)
└─ DelegationPolicy — rules the owner sets once
   ├─ Auth Rules (OR — "who is the principal?", any one may stamp)
   │    ├─ AddressAuth  ·  CapAuth (a DelegateCap-holding object = the agent)
   │    └─ ScopedAuth (attenuable sub-delegation)  ·  ZkAuth (a ZK proof)
   └─ Caveat Rules (AND — "is this spend allowed?", all must stamp)
        ├─ BudgetRule (per-coin / per-service cap)  ·  RecipientAllowlist
        └─ ScopedBudget (per-cap budget)  ·  TimeWindow  ·  OraclePriceRule
`Here’s why the design holds up, and why it fits Sui specifically:
A grant is three things at once: a budget, a list of protocols it can touch, and a revoke switch. “Up to 500 USDC, DeepBook only” is a typed cap, not a blanket approval. The agent can’t go over its ceiling because the chain rejects anything that does.
The spend request works like a hot potato. It’s a Move object that can’t be dropped, and every rule has to stamp it or the whole transaction fails. A rule reads the request but can’t change it, so it can block a spend but can never rewrite the amount or the recipient.
Revoking is one step: bump the policy version and every active grant dies at once. An agent is a sub-account with a budget, not a key, so taking back its access never touches your vault.
Every grant, revoke, and spend emits an event, so the agent’s full history stays on-chain and auditable.
The system is built on Sui’s SIP-58 custody pattern and follows the shape of ERC-7710, 8004, and 8183. It’s live on mainnet: wallet, policy, cap_auth, sub_delegate, and intent, plus a permissionless policy_rule_registry where anyone can publish a new rule.

### **3. The Agent: A Built-In AI CFO With a Guardian**

You state a goal in plain language. The agent pulls live data, turns it into a Sui transaction, and shows you the risks in readable terms before anything gets signed: slippage, route quality, stale pools, concentration. You read a human-readable preview and confirm. Nothing runs without you.
The agent’s abilities come in three tiers, and the UI labels each one:

- Research and read-only. Nobody signs. The agent checks token prices, DeFi data like TVL and APY, chart structure, and vault holdings.

- Owner actions. The agent drafts the transaction and you sign it with your own wallet. This covers creating a vault, vault actions, agent setup, and adapter authorization.

Agent execution. The agent key signs, bounded by the on-chain caps. This covers swaps, multi-step actions, adapter-integrated actions, and general spends.
A few things keep the loop trustworthy. The agent won’t quote a price, route, or APY without live data, and it flags the risk before you sign, so it never invents IDs or numbers. A whole sequence, swap then lend then leverage, even hiring another agent, can run as a single atomic Programmable Transaction Block, and every step passes the same policy gate. Within its budget the agent runs the strategy itself, including live DeepBook orders, and the chain enforces its ceiling.

### **4. Adapters: The Skills an Agent Can Run**

Most of what an agent does maps to an on-chain adapter, which works **like a skill for a Sup agent. **Each adapter is a small Move package (around 30 lines) that teaches the agent one protocol action, so the agent only ever does what an adapter explicitly allows. Authorization is set at the coin level. It’s revocable at any time and recorded on chain.
The set of adapters is open for any protocols. We use verified publisher mechenics to let users tell which adapters are reliable. We also provide AI tools and an SDK so protocols can wire their own adapters onto Sup, and the Adapter Marketplace lets users add a wide range of adapters safely. To start, the Sup team ships a few featured ones:

- Cetus for DEX and liquidity: swap aggregator, CLMM, and DLMM.

- Scallop, NAVI, Suilend, and Current for lending: supply and borrow.

- Bucket for a stablecoin CDP: USDB borrowing and savings.

- Haedal for liquid staking: SUI to haSUI and back.

- DeepBook for the on-chain order book: margin manager and live orders.

- Bluefin for perps and spot: Pro perp margin and CLMM liquidity.

- Aftermath for multiple asset classes: perps and AFLP vaults.

Because each adapter is small and self-contained, the catalogue grows fast, and the agent gains a new skill every time one ships.

### **5. Where You Can Use Sup**

Sup runs on the web and on mobile, and both share the same agent backend.
The web app is the full workbench: vault, agent chat, activity log, an adapter marketplace, and a manager for your policies and delegates. It also includes Strategy Studio, with live charts, SMC/SNR drawing tools, a Monaco editor for writing SupScript strategies, and backtesting. A deployed strategy runs inside the same typed, capped allowances as everything else. The web app also handles recurring subscriptions and on-chain inheritance, which works as a capped dead-man’s switch. It supports multiple models, multiple networks, and two languages.
The mobile app, built in React Native, is a self-custodial wallet you carry with you. It shows your portfolio, runs the same AI agent, and supports tap-to-pay and QR. It includes an embedded perp DEX with a Quick Ticket, the adapter marketplace with coin-level grants, an in-app dApp browser, and on-device backtesting. Signing is native, unlock is biometric, and screenshots are blocked.

### **6. Onboarding: Three Ways In, No Seed Phrase Exposed**

You can get into Sup three ways, and none of them leak your seed phrase.

- zkLogin with one-tap Google. This uses an Enoki ZK proof and an ephemeral key on your device. The same Google account gives you the same Sui address on every device, and you never touch a mnemonic.

- Sign-In-With-Sui. This is a standard wallet signature, and on mobile it’s signed silently in the background. Every agent route requires it.

Seed phrase or private-key import. This supports BIP-44 or a single key, protected by a password and biometrics, and your keys never leave the device.
After that, the Vault wizard turns the hard part, defining what the agent can do, into a few taps. You pick a risk profile, authorize the matching adapters in one batch with their budget and coin caps, enable the agent, and you’re done. A first-time user can finish in minutes and walk away with an agent that has a budget, a sandbox, and a revoke button.

### **8. An Agent That Hires Another Agent**

Most permission systems are flat. You grant access, and the holder either has it or doesn’t. Sup’s delegation nests instead. An agent can hand a narrower slice of its own budget to another agent, and authority only shrinks as it moves down the chain. No layer can pass on more than it was given.
This is what lets agents work with other agents safely. Suppose your agent needs a task done and hires a second agent for it. It funds the job from its own capped budget, routing the money through a SpendRequest into escrow. The escrow pays out only when an assessor rule confirms the work was delivered, and the spend still counts against the cap you originally set.

`Owner's vault
  → managed agent (DelegateCap + capped budget)
     → sub-delegates a smaller budget to a second agent
        → funds the job through a SpendRequest into escrow
           ├─ begin_spend (anyone can trigger)
           ├─ assessor rule stamps on "delivery complete"
           └─ confirm_spend → pays out to the second agent
`Revoke once at the top and the whole chain dies with it, however many layers deep it went.

### **9. Why Sup Works**

Delegation is a Move object, so authority is something you can prove on-chain. Revoking it means bumping one version, and every active grant dies with it.
Agents are sub-accounts, not keys. You can see exactly what an agent is allowed to do, pull its access back whenever you want, and your vault stays untouched.
The design fits Sui rather than just sitting on it. The policy is a Move object, onboarding runs on zkLogin, actions compose into a single transaction, and trades settle on DeepBook. Each of these is a Sui primitive doing real work in the agent loop, which is what makes the agent safer and easier to compose.
And it’s already shipped: web, mobile, MCP, and CLI on one vault, with most adapters live on mainnet.

### **Tech Stack at a Glance**

The web app runs on Next.js 15, React 19, and the Vercel AI SDK 5, which works with Claude, Gemini, and OpenAI-compatible models. It uses @mysten/sui, dApp-kit, and Enoki for zkLogin, and it’s secured by Sign-In-With-Sui, typed on-chain caps, and one-click revoke.
The mobile app runs on React Native and React 19, talks to the same agent backend over HTTP and SSE streaming, and uses @mysten/sui with native signing. It’s secured by biometrics, the Keychain, and screenshot blocking.
The contracts are written in Move on Sui: SIP-58 custody, a hot-potato policy engine, and a pluggable rule marketplace. They’re deployed on mainnet.
